            他们曾经是HR、羽毛球运动员，现在是听障咖啡师，深圳福田这间咖啡店托起“无声梦想”

                信息来源：羊城晚报
                时间：2024-08-16

                    字体：
                    [大]
                    [中]
                    [小]

                　　“我们听不见您的声音，但我们乐于为您服务，很高兴为您做咖啡……”点单区的提示牌上，一行醒目的字提示着这家咖啡店的与众不同。

近日，在深圳福田华强北，一家开在福强社区党群服务中心内的“无声咖啡”店开业。这里的店员们曾经是HR、羽毛球运动员，而今他们都是听障咖啡师。运行以来，咖啡馆受到许多热心市民的支持与好评。这家由社区联合社会组织共同打造的咖啡店，不仅是社会了解特殊群体的窗口，还是残障人士就业创业空间和筑梦舞台，帮助他们实现自我价值。 　　 　　无声咖啡馆的运行，是福田区关爱残障人的一个缩影。福田区作为深圳户籍持证残疾人最多的区域，多年来推动了不少残障人民生实事落地。 　　无声咖啡 无障碍交流

走进“无声咖啡”店，宽敞明亮的空间，简单而温馨的装饰，没有背景音乐和人声喧哗，大多数时候只会听到咖啡机磨豆子、打奶泡的声音。正在忙碌的听障咖啡师潘鹏，用手写板和顾客交流所需的口味。几分钟后，递上一杯香气四溢的咖啡，竖起大拇指表示“谢谢”。 　　这家“无声咖啡”店里，包括潘鹏在内的7名工作人员均是听障人士，他们从2020年开始跟随主理人杨元成创业，经历过小吃店、果汁店，于2023年展开咖啡店的经营，如今这家“无声咖啡”已是他们在华强北的第二家门店。 　　为了切身了解这些听障人士，记者和他们通过文字“聊”起了天。来自安徽的潘鹏，今年26岁，是“无声咖啡”店的店长，原先在上海做人力资源，2018年经朋友介绍来到深圳创业，而后深圳的包容度让他毅然决定扎根这里。“因为热爱咖啡，所以选择了这份职业。”潘鹏在写字板上写道，入职“无声咖啡”，让他结识了许多朋友，这份工作也让他能够自食其力。 　　 　　和潘鹏一样发生变化的还有店员王孟兴，她曾是羽毛球队的运动员，为未来转型而加入这个有前景的团队。“我很喜欢这里的工作环境，能和这里的同事交流很温暖、很美好。”王孟兴告诉记者，对他们而言，和同为听障人士的朋友一起工作、交流是一件难能可贵的事情。当需要与顾客沟通时，店内的点单标识、小黑板、智能语音系统等都可以帮助他们进行无障碍交流。 　　“让我最为开心的是，很多顾客点咖啡之余很喜欢和我们学手语，跟我们分享生活。”潘鹏表示，咖啡店为他们打开了一个与社会交流、市民沟通的双向通道，“比起同情和怜悯，我们更希望得到平视和尊重，让更多人知道我们听障人士有能力，也可以做事。” 　　凝聚温情 造血式帮扶

这样一个凝聚着温情的咖啡店背后，离不开创始人杨元成的努力，他是这个团队中唯一的健全人，被店员们亲切地称为“老杨”。 　　起初，老杨在一家餐饮店认识了这群孩子，机缘巧合之下带领着他们创业。2019年，老杨开始组建一个全是听障人士的团队，“我们不希望他们躲在家里面‘等、靠、要’，而希望他们能独立面对社会。”此后无论是外卖店还是果汁店、咖啡店，他们都用行动证明着“有那么一群特殊的人，用自己的劳力换取他们的报酬。” 　　比起“输血式”提供就业机会，老杨更想做的是“造血式”的帮助。他希望每个从全国各地来到深圳的听障店员都能在工作的过程中学习到足以独立运营水吧的能力，到自己的家乡去再开一家咖啡店，以此形成对听障人士的辐射性帮扶。 　　距“无声咖啡”不远处，位于梅林街道的“喜憨儿洗车中心”，一群具有心智障碍的孩子们也拥有了自己稳定的工作。2015年，曹军经由众筹在深圳福田创办了国内第一家“喜憨儿洗车中心”，最初由8名心智障碍的孩子担任技工。 　　他们经过指导老师的专业培训，逐渐能够完美地完成自己的工作任务。曹军和培训老师又根据心智障碍程度匹配合适的洗车工序，实现了效率最大化。目前“喜憨儿洗车中心”的效率已达到洗车市场的普遍需求。 　　“正常收费，谢绝小费”八个贴在门口的大字，昭示着每一个残障人士都应该有尊严地活着，用劳动换取应得的报酬。从最初的8人到如今推广到全国42家洗车中心，“喜憨儿洗车中心”成功实现400名特殊人群的稳定就业。 　　“喜憨儿洗车中心”和“无声咖啡”一样，成为残障人士链接社会的窗口。 　　弱有众扶 公益在行动

残障人士的公益道路上，社会多方力量的帮扶功不可没。老杨提到他很感谢福强社区、基金会对这群孩子的帮扶。他们不仅仅为“无声咖啡”免费提供场地，还引入德义慈善机构“益+小站”。在政府的宣传下，有源源不断的顾客慕名而来。 　　据华强北街道福强社区有关负责人介绍，“无声咖啡”是企业助力“百千万工程”共建共营项目之一，以“党建+服务”“公益+运营”的模式，打造了全市首家在党群服务中心内的公益咖啡店。店内不仅有座位，还为路过的快递员、外卖员、环卫工人提供免费的柠檬水。 　　“无声咖啡”店的场地、水电由福强社区免费提供，无声咖啡店总营收5%反哺给社区基金，用于助困扶弱及开展公益活动，实现“自我造血”。社区党委也将不定期组织公益手语培训，提升听障人士价值感。在给居民提供低偿饮品服务的同时，还能给听障人士提供就业岗位，形成党建引领、公益先行、低于市场、普惠居民的可持续、高质量服务机制。 　　事实上，福田区作为深圳户籍持证残疾人最多的区域，一直以来格外关注残障人士的民生实事。一方面，福田区率先建设了全区救助帮扶智慧系统；另一方面，全覆盖走访特殊困难群体，全面动态掌握困难群众的需求信息。目前，国家级残障重点研究中心首个创新实践基地已落户福田。 　　授人以鱼，更要授人以渔。近年来，福田区不断完善残疾人职业培训基地建设，目前已推动了一系列对残障人士的创新性帮扶项目：高标准打造“福康之家”弱有众扶专项示范点；率先在深圳首家星巴克手语咖啡店落地首个扶残助残关爱空间；开展“走访拓岗促就业”专项行动，开拓残障人就业机会和岗位……与此同时，不断扩大助残“朋友圈”，撬动社会力量，在全区逐渐构建起“大联合、大关爱、大服务”的助残服务体系，促进残障人全面发展，擦亮“首善之区”的幸福底色。

百年新华路上全国首家无障碍酒馆悄然落地（青年报·无碍理想运营与活动）

来源：http://mp.weixin.qq.com/s?__biz=MjM5MzY4ODcyMA==&mid=2658313843&idx=1&sn=9ac7a3f963a2d1f8b61b499fdd2dac0d&chksm=bc4f78564768fbcbf3b3674c1694c9f69a9f12ffdfaab7e7f3d76e5194482b334601ffb88b57#rd 抓取时间：2026-11-09

全国首家！无障碍酒馆落地上海

                                  原创

                                  青春上海
                青春上海

            青春上海

            在小说阅读器读本章

        去阅读

              在公众号小说中沉浸阅读

百年新华路上 全国首家无障碍酒馆悄然落地 隐于“新华FICS365”园区一隅 代替台阶的平缓斜坡 全是圆角的桌子 以及丰富多彩的活动 …… 让我们一起走进这家 名为“无碍理想”的酒馆看看吧

宽敞的门扉 墙上的盲文地图 白板上的手语绘图 酒馆的90后主理人夏玉杰 不仅在硬件上打造真正的无障碍 更希望通过“handycup”的理念 让每个人都能轻松举杯 他的无碍理想是 重建融合的生活

“无碍”细节在宽敞的门扉、盲文地图

FICS新华365园区的前身是花园洋房 上海搪瓷研究所以及东华大学学生宿舍楼 拥有深厚的文化和历史底蕴 经过改建后今年正式对外开放 在园区里拐过几个弯 “无碍理想”的灯牌散发着明黄色的光 酒馆不到30平方米 却处处透着“无碍”

门口没有台阶，出入口的大门宽约1.1米，比一般店铺的大门宽了20厘米，特意用了折叠推拉门，肢体障碍者就算坐在轮椅上也能单手推开。环顾店内，酒馆设有低吧台，贴的标签配有盲文，还设计出一幅按比例缩小的盲文版室内布局图张贴在靠墙的软木板上，方便视障伙伴们了解室内的格局和桌椅的摆放。

墙上的白板是手语角，绘有“尊重”“平等”“做自己”等手语表达，也能让听障人士用文字书写表达自己。蹲下看看门旁边的书架，放置的桌游也是无障碍游戏。

酒馆提供10种精酿啤酒 不定期更换 夏玉杰一般每天傍晚6点 开始在酒馆忙碌 而下午的营业会交给搭档刘慧

这天吧台边坐着夏玉杰开酒馆后新认识的朋友磊磊，磊磊是名听障人士，自从知道“无碍理想”酒馆营业后，他周末有空就来喝一杯。磊磊先为自己点了一杯4号“再见杏福”，一起来的朋友最后试了9号“酒花轰炸”，竖起了大拇指。俩人各自喝着酒，还不忘向吧台后忙着打酒的夏玉杰点头，夏玉杰则用开业后学的简单手语和他们交流，开心时还一起干杯。

大家如此放松 让夏玉杰感到一丝欣慰 就像他会和很多人解释 “无碍理想”的英文名是HandyCup 由handicap（残障）一词变化而来 寓意让所有人都能轻松举杯

因为感同身受，他想打造社交空间

开一家“无障碍”店铺的想法在夏玉杰心中由来已久。中学时期，因为一次医疗事故导致夏玉杰的左手萎缩，失去了正常功能，此后他开始了解无障碍文化，观察身边无障碍环境，甚至去国外留学时，他观察最多的也是无障碍设施。

毕业后，夏玉杰成为一名商业律师，既是因为当时遇到医疗事故时得到过热心律师的帮助，更是想用律师的专业知识在无障碍方面为残障伙伴做些什么。

夏玉杰表达能力极好 先前一直参与活动、工作坊 和普通大众交流 让无障碍理念触达更多人 随着参与越深 他越觉得 残障人士也需要一个 鼓励他们能走出家门的社交空间

“无障碍的基础是多元融合 最好的融合是社交 酒馆就是一个很自然的社交空间”

去年年底 他和搭档刘慧一起去西藏、甘肃旅游 在珠峰脚下遇到一家残障者开的民宿 对方将民宿打理得很好 触动了夏玉杰的心弦 “回上海后就决定开无障碍酒馆了”

选择落地新华路 是因为在参加相关活动时 夏玉杰感受到这里的包容 装修也是挑战 很多设计师并没设计过无障碍空间 夏玉杰参考了 《中华人民共和国无障碍环境建设法》 等法律法规 还找到不同情况的残障伙伴 了解无障碍需求

“比如视障朋友给出了实用的建议 把所有的桌子都磨成圆角 避免被磕碰 听障朋友遇到过助听器没有电的情况 于是有朋友捐了助听器电池 现在就放在与白板绑定的一次性杯子里 供大家使用”

今年5月20日 “无碍理想”酒馆正式开业 夏玉杰在自己的朋友圈发布了酒馆开业的消息 大家口口相传 小酒馆人气也越来越高 目前酒馆离不开夏玉杰 他更承担了无障碍融合的解答宣传工作 本职的律师工作暂处于空档期

“无碍”的生活空间让更多人“有爱”

当残障人士愿意走出家门来到酒馆后 夏玉杰思考起如何宣传无障碍理念 分享和交流无疑是个好方法 在前期承担了不少分享工作后 夏玉杰将主导权逐渐交给了客人们

来自浦东的视障者毛毛成为主讲人之一 在“无碍理想”开了三期盲文科普活动 从认识盲文、中英盲文的特点 到教授汉语盲文拼写 他将自己作为盲人最深刻的体验分享给大家 让更多的人了解了盲人文化 也学会如何与盲人相处

而夏玉杰则忙前忙后 为毛毛制作海报，做前期宣发 “三场讲座反馈很好 还有普通市民参加 跟着毛毛学习了简单的盲文拼写 我想让他们认识到自己的优势 不要害怕交流，也不要消沉”

手语、绘画疗愈工作坊 听障摄影师个人展、视障伙伴调酒 一人一故事剧场 …… 一系列的活动有条不紊地进行 在10月举行的第二届新华·社区营造大会上 小酒馆还成为其中一个分会场 夏玉杰面对更多人做了分享

对于酒馆的未来 夏玉杰还有更多设想 比如硬件方面 想把店内的小方桌改成可升降式 方便照顾更多群体 他还在社交平台上发布了无碍主理人的招募 运营一日酒馆 策划无障碍包容理念的活动 也结识有趣的朋友

我要做的就是把不同背景的人拉到一起 让他们看到还有其他不一样的人 再群策群力 如何让共同的生活空间 对多元个体更友好、更有爱

你听说过无障碍理念吗？ 支持这样的无障碍酒馆吗？

        FAQ

                            1.什么是“黑暗中对话”？


            黑暗中对话（中国）社会企业（DID）是德国“对话社会企业”在中国唯一授权机构，2010年9月正式进入中国。自1988年在德国首创以来，DID已在36个国家和地区落户，现时运营有17个永久体验馆。DID提供让参与者在完全暗黑的环境中进行体验或培训，透过视觉转变的场景和活动设计，为视障人士创造能够发挥其才能的就业机会，同时帮助社会大众提升对自我，他人和团队的认知，进而帮助消除社会偏见，促进多元和社会文化。




            2. 什么是“社会企业”？


            社会企业旨在以创新的方式解决社会问题。它通过商业手法运行，并将收入贡献给社会，即社会企业可以通过自我运营，将所得收入用于扶助弱势群体等社会公益事业，促进社会发展和社会和谐。社会企业在中国是一种较新颖的概念，但已日益引起社会关注。




            3.黑暗中对话是一个公益组织，体验馆为什么要售票？


            黑暗中对话不仅仅是一个公益组织，还是一个能自我运营的社会企业。体验馆通过收取门票或举办活动来实现本身的自我运营，这是一种自我造血的方式，所得若有盈利，将回馈给社会公益事业，如视障者的教育或培训等。




            4.黑暗中对话体验馆是什么？


            “黑暗中对话”体验发生在全黑的环境中。参观者们通常以8人为一组，在简短的介绍之后使用手杖踏上黑暗的征程。在黑暗中对话导赏员的指导和陪同下，参观者们将用75-90分钟的时间视觉以外的其他感官去探索精心设计的黑暗展区：游览公园，走过繁华的街市，穿过市场，经过咖啡馆等等。在这个全新的体验中，参观者的世界被打开，并重新定义。视障人群所处的黑暗世界，并不会更差——只是有所不同。


            黑暗中对话成都体验馆是中国大陆第一家体验馆，全馆面积约为960平方米。该馆二楼为全黑体验区，既包含全球黑暗中对话体验馆的特色，又融入了成都的地方特色，也是目前在全球唯一一个完全以城市为主题展开的体验馆。




            5. 导赏员是些什么人？


            所有的导赏员都是失明或弱视人士，受聘于黑暗中的对话，并接受过专业的训练。他们陪伴参观者完成整个体验，并帮助参观者将这样的经历投射到日常生活中。他们在黑暗中行动自如，游刃有余地处理每个人的需求，确保在体验过程中游客舒适和安全的。导赏员将引领一个富有想象力，寓教于乐的体验，让体验者突破焦虑和迷茫，通过其他感官感知世界的喜悦！




            6.体验馆禁止携带什么？


            为确保所有参加者能充分体验旅程的感受，以及保障体验旅程的质素及完整性，参加者禁止携带以下物品进入体验区：


            a. 手提袋／背包


            b. 手机 (必须存放于储物柜)


            c. 任何形式的摄影或录影设备 d.饮食或咀嚼口香糖


            e. 会发光的装置、手表、珠宝、萤光运动鞋或手提/平版电脑等


            f. 电动轮椅 (本馆可提供手动轮椅以供于场馆内使用，但需提前2天预约告知需求)


            g. 眼镜于场馆内将不能发挥用途，并有可能跌落及摔破。若参加者坚持配戴眼镜进入场地，本馆将不承担任何相关责任。本馆建议参加者进入体验旅程前先将眼镜放入储物柜内。




            7. 黑暗中对话已和哪些企业举办过工作坊？


            目前黑暗中对话已为世界500强中超过150家企业举办了“黑暗中对话”工作坊。


            部分合作伙伴包括：中国银行，阿里巴巴，腾讯，新浪，SAP公司，博世集团，NPI公益组织，智享会商务服务有限公司，上海通用汽车公司，中欧国际工商学院，凯德商用，工银瑞信，星展银行等。




            8. 黑暗中对话到过哪些国家，主要城市？


            亚非：


            中国 – 上海，成都，香港，台北


            印度－海德拉巴


            以色列－霍隆


            日本－大阪、东京


            韩国－首尔


            马来西亚－吉隆坡


            新加坡


            泰国－曼谷


            欧洲：


            奥地利－维也纳


            德国－法兰克福、汉堡、慕尼黑


            意大利－热那亚、米兰


            俄罗斯－莫斯科


            希腊 – 雅典


            土耳其 – 伊斯坦布尔


            美洲：阿根廷－布宜诺斯艾利斯




            9. 什么是黑暗中对话公益基金？


            黑暗中对话（中国）基于其包容、认知和赋能的社会使命，设立黑暗中对话公益基金，用于推动和拓展中国视障人群的平等教育就业机会。该基金将与商业企业及社会组织合作，从视障者的实际需求出发以项目形式独立透明运营。黑暗中对话公益基金使用情况将接受社会大众的监督。




            10. 为什么要携带十元零钱？


            十元将在体验馆的体验旅程中使用，与体验中的一个环节有关，使体验增加趣味性和真实感。




            11. 助行工具手杖的使用方法？


            a. 将手杖顶端绳扣在手腕上，调节它的松紧程度，让自己感觉舒适。这样做是为了当你不小心松手的时候或者用手去触摸探索的时候，手杖不会掉落在地上。


            b. 手握住手柄，手肘靠近身体，让手杖在身体前方四十五度角左右移动探索空间和障碍物。需要强调的是，手杖是你探索的工具，在完全黑暗的空间内，请听从导赏员的指示和要求，以保证自身和同伴的安全。请一定不要将手杖朝上举起，或挥舞，或用力敲打，这样也是为了保护场馆的设施。


            12. 体验者应遵守的守则：


            a. 参加者若为12岁或以下儿童建议由成人陪同进入体验馆内。本馆不建议8岁以下及或身高低于95cm的儿童参加此活动。


            b. 由于体验馆的独特设计，恕不接待逾时到达的参加者。而所售出的门票恕不退换或退款。


            c. 参加者如不遵守本馆的守则因而引致任何人身损伤或财物的损失，本馆无须负上相关的责任。


            d. 本馆导赏员鼓励参加者透过触摸及探索来进行体验旅程，惟禁止移动馆内任何物品。如不遵守本馆的守则而引致馆内的物品损坏，该参加者须照价赔偿。


            e. 参加者在漆黑环境中会面对一定的风险，故不建议怀孕妇女参加此活动。


            f. 60岁以上老人建议家人陪同。


            g. 心脏病，幽闭恐惧症患者等不建议参加。


            h. 女士建议穿平底鞋。




            13. 关于体验馆摄影问题：


            本馆允许于接待大堂进行私人性质的摄影及录影，但全黑场地内不能拍照、录影或录音。商业用途的摄影或录影须事先得到场馆有关部门的批准。参加者进入本馆后已代表本馆有权采用参加的相片或录像以作教育或宣传用途。


            15. 体验馆针对特殊人群的优惠政策是怎样？


            学生，残疾人，六十周岁以上老年人参观实行半票；视障人士参观实行免票。


            父母携带一位未成年子女，可享受家庭套票，未成年子女免一位票价




            14. 体验馆方便停车吗？


            团队购票请预先告知车位需求。


            15. 黑暗中对话提供哪些活动？怎么参加？


            黑暗中对话目前已经开发出包括黑暗中对话体验馆和黑暗中对话工作坊、黑暗中咖啡、暗中品酒、暗中音乐会、暗中生日会、暗中派对、暗中舞台剧等基于“黑暗”平台的丰富的产品，让大众来领略“黑暗”的力量。


            具体合作请邮件 info@dialogue-in-the-dark.org.cn

            Three ways to change a vendor's life this Christmas

            From London to the World – How The Big Issue went global

            There are now more than 100 street papers offering a hand up to tens of thousands of homeless men and women worldwide

                        by:
                                                        Andrew Burns

                        27 Oct 2016

                Share

    It started in London in 1991 with a single magazine.

Twenty-five years on, The Big Issue has gone global with a series of sister titles in Australia, South Africa, Japan, Taiwan and Korea, and more than 100 fellow street papers – from Budapest to Buenos Aires and Melbourne to Mexico City – across 41 countries.

Each publication is linked through the Glasgow-based International Network of Street Papers, which The Big Issue co-founded in 1994. Here are just a few…

BIG ISSUE AUSTRALIA

First published: June 1996, in Melbourne

Frequency: fortnightly

Vendors buy copies for $3.50 and sell for $7

Number of vendors: 650-700

Around 6,500 people have sold more than 10 million copies of the magazine since it was first published in Melbourne.

BIG ISSUE SOUTH AFRICA

First published: December 1996

Frequency: monthly

Vendors buy copies for R12.50 and sell to the public for R25

Number of vendors: 250-300

Set up after The Body Shop Foundation gave a grant for a feasibility study, over the last 20 years the magazine has helped thousands of vendors earn more than £1.2m.

Over the last 20 years the magazine has helped thousands of vendors earn more than £1.2m

BIG ISSUE JAPAN

First published: September 2003, in Osaka

Frequency: fortnightly

Vendors buy copies for 170 yen and sell for 350 yen

Number of vendors: 150

With vendors in 13 major Japanese cities, in its first 10 years The Big Issue Japan sold six million copies, earning £6.3m for its vendors.

Advertising helps fund Big Issue’s mission to end poverty

Advertising helps fund Big Issue’s mission to end poverty

BIG ISSUE TAIWAN

First published: April 2010 in Taipei

Frequency: monthly

Vendors buy each copy for NT$50 and sell for NT$100

Number of vendors: Around 60 sellers in Taipei and New Taipei City

BIG ISSUE KOREA

First published: July 2010

Frequency: fortnightly

Vendors buy copies for 2,500 won and sell it on the street for 5,000 won

Number of vendors: 2,000

The youngest addition to The Big Issue global family, The Big Issue Korea helps its vendors in Seoul boost their confidence through weekly ballet classes

Other street papers across the world…

                                Keep reading the journalism that powers our mission

                                                                Register free to continue and learn more about the stories, campaigns and the support we provide, helping people facing poverty across the UK.




                                By registering, you agree to our Terms & Conditions and Privacy Policy.

                                or

                    I will register later

SHEDIA, GREECE

As the Greek financial crisis rocked Europe in 2013, Shedia rose from the streets of Athens and continues to offer hope and a new way of thinking for the embattled people in the Greek capital. Shedia follows the same model as The Big Issue. Vendors buy magazines for €1.50 and sell them for €3.

MI VALEDOR, MEXICO CITY

First published in February 2015, Mi Valedor was launched by Maria Portilla in the Mexican capital after a visit to The Big Issue’s offices on a trip to the UK. Mi Valedor is ‘my pal’ in Mexican slang, but Maria says that its meaning translates to “my protector or someone who looks out for me”.

REAL CHANGE, SEATTLE

After the pioneering investment from London Big Issue vendor Simon Mott to start accepting card payments from his pitch, Seattle-based Real Change developed an innovative smartphone app last year to help vendors cater to increasingly cashless customers. Seattle residents can now buy the magazine for $2.99 (digital edition) direct from street vendors.

The monthly publication is sold outside Metro stations around St Petersburg by 16 vendors

PUT DOMOI,ST PETERSBURG, RUSSIA

For 22 years Put Domoi has been helping homeless people earn an income. The only street paper operating in Russia, the monthly publication is sold outside Metro stations around St Petersburg by 16 vendors, most of whom are over 60 and mentally or physically disabled. It helps them source medical treatment and support. Approximately 50,000 people in St Petersburg are homeless, with around 5,000 rough sleepers.

MEGAFON, BERGEN

One of Norway’s five street papers, Megafon’s vendors offer a pay-by-text service as well as cash transactions. Selling for 100 kroner, vendors keep half the cover price. Norwegian street papers recently banded together to discuss how drug addiction and mental health problems – two of the biggest issues affecting their vendors – can be tackled.

HECHO EN BUENOS AIRES, ARGENTINA

Launched in June 2000, Hecho en Buenos Aires is a multi-award-winning news and arts magazine that has hard-hitting coverage of social issues alongside creative work from vendors themselves. It sells for $20 of which vendors keep $13.

Advertising helps fund Big Issue’s mission to end poverty

流浪者就业caseBigIssue大阪店-JapanTimes

Big Issue finds home; homeless in charge（Japan Times·大阪政府免租店铺）

来源：https://www.japantimes.co.jp/news/2010/10/02/national/big-issue-finds-home-homeless-in-charge/ 抓取方式：搜索摘要存档（原站拒绝抓取），2026-11-09

The Big Issue Japan Ltd., a publisher that hires homeless people to sell its magazine on the streets, opened on Friday Japan's first shop managed by homeless people in a former convenience store in Nishi-Umeda subway station.

The company is using the shop rent-free for a year, thanks to the Osaka Municipal Government, which used to run it. Twelve homeless people will manage it, four at a time every four months, Big Issue Japan spokesman Koichi Yoshida said. "It's very rare that homeless people operate an outlet. I have never heard of this anywhere in the world," Yoshida said.

Big Issue Japan's business model — hiring homeless people for its sales force — was first introduced in London in 1991. Its Japanese-language magazine, issued twice a month, was launched in September 2003.

The magazine costs ¥300, with the sales staff keeping ¥160 for themselves.

Having a shop is a great advantage because it can display back issues, sales aren't affected by bad weather, and customers always know where they can pick up the magazine, Yoshida said.

Big Issue Japan sells about 30,000 copies per issue, resulting in ¥18 million a month in sales, President Shoji Sano said. The company has sold about 4.1 million magazines since 2003 and the homeless have earned a total of ¥550 million, he said.

A total of 1,150 homeless people have registered as sales staff, 40 percent of whom stay on longer than a month. Of the 1,150, 135 have "graduated" to other jobs, and 150 are currently selling the magazine in 15 prefectures, he said.

流浪者就业caseBigIssue日本-官网英文版

What is the Big Issue Japan?（官网英文版）

来源：https://www.bigissue.jp/english/index.html 抓取时间：2026-11-09

What is the Big Issue Japan?乥THE BIG ISSUE JAPAN

    Japanese

          What is the Big Issue Japan?

          About The company

          Vendors

          The Big Issue Japan Foundation

          Links to The Big Issue in the world

    仾The Big Issue Japan

Foundation

      The Big Issue Japan is a business response to a social problem: homelessness.

Established in 2003, The Big Issue Japan publishes a high-quality magazine that is sold by homeless people.

      Having proved they are homeless, vendors are given ten free copies of the magazine to sell. After that they buy each copy for 170 yen and sell it for 350 yen.

      The Big Issue Japan seeks to help the homeless help themselves offering a hand-up rather than a hand-out.

By working as a vendor, a homeless person can earn income and learn to manage his/her budget. The work also provides the chance to reconnect with the society and most importantly to regain self-confidence and sense of trust.

Working as a social business, The Big Issue Japan helps vendors recover their independence and self-respect while gaining business and management skills.

      From September 2003 to March 2013, 1427 homeless people had registered with the Big Issue Japan as vendors. By March 2013, 5.71million copies of the Big Issue Japan were sold in Japan which provided a total of 802 million yen in income to homeless vendors.

  Company乥Advertising

      Sano-san -- A Man with a Vision

  Submitted by Japan Inc on December 15, 2005

The Big Issue Japan takes on the bigger issue of Japanese homelessness.

Emily Kubo

Our map leads us to an unassuming residential apartment building, tucked away in a quiet side street in Takadanobaba. Unsure of ourselves, Endo-san, our photographer, finds a small sign on a rusted mailbox and we hesitantly knock on the door. Entering the tiny one-room studio, we are immediately confronted with boxes of magazines and marketing materials. A young man in his late 20s sporting a Big Issue Japan T-shirt, jeans and house slippers greets us sheepishly and invites us to sit on metal chairs around a diminutive square table. An exceptionally tall and attractive young woman soon enters the room and makes us tea as we await their boss.

Ten minutes later we are greeted with traditional bowing and a business card exchange by the man that we came to meet -- Shoji Sano, publisher of The Big Issue Japan, a street magazine sold by homeless people in the UK, Australia, South Africa, and most recently, Japan. As Sano joins us at our table, the young staffers retreat to the other end of the room (albeit just a feet away). They sit at their conjoining desks and plug away at their computers. We later find out that the young man by the name of Tsuyoshi Katori was previously Music Editor at the popular Tokyo Walker magazine. The young woman, Mariko Ikeda, who has an interesting background of her own, is an ex-fashion model from Nagano who never saw a homeless person until she came to Tokyo. It is obvious that they work here not for any financial incentive or the glamour of it, but for the love of their jobs alone.

Background

Shoji Sano, born and raised in Osaka, never imagined that one day he would travel to Tokyo once a month, at age 65, to begin his new life as an entrepreneur. But in fact, at an age when most would be enjoying retired life and a well-earned nenkin, or government pension, Sano spends his time traveling between two major cities as publisher of The Big Issue Japan. "I never imagined that I would work in Tokyo," says Sano, who has an articulate and commanding presence despite his small stature.

Sano was working as a researcher for urban planning in Osaka when he realized that he wanted to do something to solve the homeless problem in his city: "There was not a single day during my commute to work that I did not encounter a homeless person," he recalls. This is readily imaginable. According to government records, there are about 25,000 homeless people in Japan, with more than 10,000 "living" in Osaka (proper and prefecture). Osaka, with a population of 2.6 million, has more than one-third of the nation's homeless. "Because my job was city planning, my perspective was to approach homelessness as a city-wide and regional problem. The homeless are attracted to cities, and they are homeless in the cities. Therefore, I viewed the homeless as another urban problem that I must try to find solutions for."

At that time, there were no policies tackling the root of homelessness; existing government measures only dealt with the problem through welfare. Sano thought differently: "The fundamental solution is providing employment. The average age of a homeless person is about 56, which makes it difficult for them to find work." Lack of jobs for older citizens is indeed not just a social issue for homeless people. "If you are over 50 and do not have a job in Japan, you are out of the system. Of course, if you do not have a home and a family to support you, you can just imagine the hardships with getting back on your feet. That is why I thought if you do not combine welfare with job creation, you cannot solve the problem of homelessness."

Armed with this insight about five years ago, Sano approached the local city government with a proposal and a plan of action to reduce homelessness through job creation. But it was not met with enthusiasm: "Their response was that we cannot solve this problem at the local level. This is too big for us. It must be addressed at the national level." The government's fear was that its becoming the first local government with a job creation program would draw even more homeless people. "I thought it was actually a very good plan," Sano laughs, "but they basically said, 'No thank you!'"

Faced with rejection from the government, Sano then took his proposal to the private sector. His logic was that corporations should take some responsibility, since many of the homeless had been workers in their companies until the restructuring in the 1990s. In fact, unlike many of the homeless in the West, Japanese homeless in general do not suffer from psychological disorders or drug addiction, and have a genuine desire to work and get back on track. "But they said to me," recounts Sano, "'our company did not lay off anyone -- so why should we chip in and underwrite other companies' restructuring? This is not our problem.'"

Sano's proposal was turned down by both local government and local companies. And so the buck was passed.

But like a true entrepreneur, Sano decided to tackle the problem from another angle. "Yes, I failed as a professional," he says. "So I started thinking, why not tackle this social problem from the grass-roots, as a private citizen?"

How It All Began

Sano started an Osaka study group for people with an equal interest in the problem. It was through this study group that he had a fateful encounter with the future co-founder of The Big Issue Japan, Yoko Mizukoshi, an energetic woman 10 years his junior who had heard about the success of street magazines sold by homeless people in London. Fascinated by the concept, she made a side trip to Scotland while in the UK on business, to meet with Mel Young, founder of The Big Issue Scotland. At the end of their three-hour conversation, Mizukoshi casually mentioned the actual number of homeless people in Osaka. "What? 10,000 people homeless just in Osaka!" Young exclaimed. "Then why aren't you starting this magazine there? I will help you if you do!"

Young had reason to be surprised. There are only around 600 - 700 homeless people in the entire UK. In Japan, there exists about 25,000 of what the government categorizes as "rough sleepers."

In fact, Mizukoshi did not meet with Young with the intention to launch The Big Issue Japan. But the more she learned from him, the more interested she became. When prompted by the Scotsman to do something for the homeless in her own city, Mizukoshi replied, mae muki kento shimasu ("We'll give it careful consideration"), a stock, if polite, formulation for turning down someone without a flat no. "Mizukoshi is not a typical Japanese," recalls Sano, "because when she said that, she was being sincere!"

Mizukoshi's enthusiasm was infectious. "When she first told me about it, I did not think it would work in Japan. However, I was persuaded by her youthful energy." So a business partnership formed. Next, Sano turned to friends in publishing for advice. "They all told me that the project was doomed to fail -- 'There is not even a 99% chance of failure but a 100% chance!' they said," laughs Sano.

There were indeed many reasons for their pessimism. First, there was the decline in readership of traditional print media in general, a decline especially true of the magazine's target demographic, people under 35, who would presumably be interested in its mix of current events, celebrity news, and social issues. "The young people today are more into reading on the Internet and on their mobile phones," Sano laments. "So my friends asked, 'Who is going to read the magazine?'" Moreover, there are also problems that are culture-specific. For example, there was no rationale for street sales of magazines in Japan; these days streets are awash with giveaway magazines and newspapers. "Why would people want to pay 200 yen to buy a magazine from a homeless person when they can get something similar for free?" Sano asks. Then there is the deep-seated Japanese prejudice against homeless people. "People associate the homeless with the '3 K's,' " says Sano. He holds up the corresponding number of fingers as he enumerates these. "One, they are kitanai [dirty]; two, they are kusai [smelly], and three, kowai [scary]," he says, adding, "With all these negatives, it was no wonder my professional friends were convinced the project would fail!" Sano would find that once again, it would ultimately be the average people, not the professionals, who would support his vision. He was especially encouraged by the young women he surveyed, who saw this project as an opportunity to solve a social problem. Again, energized by youth, The Big Issue Japan was born: "Over 65 percent of our readers are women, and most of them are in their 20s. They are the reason we've kept going for the past two years."

How does The Big Issue Work?

The company has a sales staff of four, two each in Tokyo and Osaka, who supply the magazine to salesmen and lend an ear to their problems. The magazine is sold mainly in the Kanto and Kansai regions, including Tokyo, Yokohama, Kawasaki, Chiba, Kyoto, Osaka and Kobe.

The Big Issue Japan's mission is to give the homeless a job and get them off the streets. From the 200 yen price, the salesman keeps 110 yen, with 90 yen going to the company to cover expenses. If he sells about 25-30 copies per day, he can earn about 3,000 yen, enough for three bento lunches from the convenience store and a night in a flophouse. This is the first step -- to sleep with a roof over his head.

Daily sales of about 40 copies will leave 1,000 yen in his pocket after the above expenses. That amounts to 30,000 yen a month, which would allow an average saving of 200,000 yen in seven or eight months. With this money, he can rent an apartment. In fact, last year the Tokyo government introduced subsidised, low-rent (3,000 yen per month) apartments for up to 2 years. This means that a salesman now only needs to save 100 yen a day, allowing him to achieve in a shorter time the second step -- a place of his own. In addition, in Osaka, flophouses, which normally only allow you to pay daily, have begun to allow payment of one month's rent in advance so the "renter" can use the flophouse as an address. At 600 yen a day, it takes about 18,000 yen to gain the right to use an address.

Why so much emphasis on the address? "You need an address to do anything," explains Sano. "If you are an elderly person, you can receive welfare money from the government. If you are young, you can fill out a form and look for a job, which is the third step and ultimate goal. In Japan, without an address, it's as if you do not exist!"

Currently in Tokyo, each vendor sells about 20-25 copies a day -- still just shy of the 30 copies needed to be able to rent an apartment and acquire an address. In Osaka, however, salesmen have been able to manage about 25-30 copies a day. About half of the Osaka salesmen have successfully reached the second step in six months.

"One reason why I believe that Tokyo has been such a slow growth market is because people who live in Osaka are more used to the sight of homeless people -- there are so many of them. There is not the same psychological distance between average residents and the homeless in Osaka that exists in Tokyo," reasons Sano.

How is the company doing financially?

The company started with US$200,000 in capital. Sano had borrowed 60 percent of that sum from lending agencies. He and Mizukoshi had dipped into their own pockets for US$40,000 and received another US$40,000 in donations. "Originally, I had considered making The Big Issue into a public company and raise money through issuing stocks," Sano says. However, not only would that require a huge amount of paperwork, it would also require the company to earn money for shareholders -- and that is not the mission of the magazine. "Unfortunately, as a limited corporation, we do not make enough money to meet our costs. Right now we are about US$100,000 in debt," Sano admits. "Every month, we show about US$10,000-$15,000 in red ink, and I must borrow money from different sources to keep going," he sighs.

Although The Big Issue Japan accepts advertisements, on account of its image (what luxury brand would want to advertise in a magazine sold by the homeless?), plus its relatively low readership, the magazine currently attracts very few. Magazine circulation ranges from 30,000 to 40,000, depending on the season. In the torrid heat of summer, sales drop off dramatically because sellers simply cannot endure the sun for more than 30 minutes at a time.

Of course, The Big Issue Japan is not a company in which success is judged by profits. "In the past year and a half, we have given over US$1 million to the homeless community in Japan," Sano says with pride.

Sano and his business partner are not people of means, and they cannot possibly go on borrowing money forever. However, he is a determined man: "We have been doing this for about two years now. I have made a promise to myself that I would do this for at least three years. In these three years, no matter what happens, I will keep going. The challenge is to turn around our magazine next year."

The Big Issue Japan's Future

Sano isn't a man who gives up easily. He firmly believes that The Big Issue Japan does indeed have a future and can eventually break even: "We have gained a lot of experience and knowledge in the past two years that we can use to turn things around next year," he avers.

He has a three-part plan: 1) improving the sales structure to take into consideration seasonal factors and finding ways to stabilize sales throughout the year, 2) maximizing the sales potential in Tokyo, and 3) boosting advertising revenues. The company is now ramping up their profile. Despite extensive coverage by the media, the company seeks to control its own image. The first step has been to take advantage of a free advertising campaign by Ogilvy Mather Japan, in conjunction with Yokohama, to reduce homelessness in the port city south of Tokyo. The poster ad, which will be displayed in subway stations, features a homeless man in outline, first lying on the ground, next sitting upright, and finally standing as he holds up a copy of The Big Issue Japan: "This ad embodies our philosophy and mission," Sano explains. "We plan to extend the campaign to JR stations. We also hope to get a celebrity tie-up gratis. These are ways to raise our profile without spending any money."

Another step the company is taking is to increase the magazine's Japan-related content. Currently, much of the content comes from fellow members of the International Network of Street Papers (INSP). These stories tend to focus on Hollywood stars and overseas news. "We would like to feature Japanese rock bands," says Sano. "In the future, we hope to translate our articles for publication by street papers abroad."

Sano's future goals do not end simply with the financial success of the magazine: "Of course, our first priority is to keep The Big Issue Japan going and become sustainable," Sano says. "But in the future, I want to set up in conjunction with the magazine a separate company to help people who have reached the third step [renting their own apartment], with education and job training, in order for them to gain the skills to reintegrate into society."

A Social Entrepreneur?

Do you consider yourself a social entrepreneur? "I'm often asked that, especially during interviews with the media," laughs Sano, who can't conceal his pride, as I try my best to pronounce the term in Japanese. "But, for me, I don't so much think of myself as a social entrepreneur as I think of The Big Issue Japan as a social enterprise, " Sano emphasizes, visibly excited. "My definition of a social enterprise is a company that tries to challenge a social problem through private business. And yes, I'm working hard to make The Big Issue Japan one of the few influential social enterprises in Japan." JI

A Vocation Under the El

Outside the central entrance to JR Yurakucho Station, Toshio Wada, 62, holds up a copy of The Big Issue Japan. The down-at-the-heels salesman is not conspicuous, even among the passing suits and fashionable women, for there are other vendors under the elevated railway, a nether region of greasy spoons and the hard-up, a penumbra fringing the great white way of Ginza.

Wada, to hear him tell it, is blessed.

He once worked for Tokyo Shoko Research, Japan's oldest credit reporting agency. Then something happened, and he resigned. He doesn't elaborate.

Wada enjoyed his freedom until he ran out of money and found himself on the street. A Christian friend encouraged him to join the local church. He'd had no truck with Christianity, but the church provided hot meals, and, after his joining its bible study circle, a place to sleep. "When I look back," he reflects, "I think my homelessness led me to God."

He quit the church after four years in April 2004. He couldn't stand the politics or stomach behavior he felt was not "church-like." He was back on the street -- only this time he had a job, selling The Big Issue Japan.

Wada seems to have found his métier, selling a respectable 40 to 50 copies of the magazine daily. Within six months he achieved "step two," his own apartment, albeit a subsidised one that rents for 3,000 yen a month.

How close is Wada to achieving "step three," a regular job? "I'm not looking for other work," he says. "This job's a lot of fun." Pressed, he explains he enjoys "interacting with many people." Then he breaks into a broad grin, adding, "Many of my customers are pretty young women from Ginza -- that's the greatest pleasure."

He's found his calling.

-Burritt Sabin

Magazine: Magazine No 66

流浪者就业caseChangePlease-社企专访-SocialEnterpriseUK

“Forget you're a social enterprise!”（Social Enterprise UK 专访 Change Please）

来源：https://www.socialenterprise.org.uk/news-and-views/forget-youre-a-social-enterprise/ 抓取方式：WebFetch 全文，2026-11-09

Social Enterprise of the Year award winners Change Please tell us how they've achieved growth and impact during nearly eleven years in business.

You can't miss the headquarters of Social Enterprise of the Year award winners Change Please, the coffee company that tackles homelessness. Outside the commercial unit they occupy on a small trading estate in Peckham in south London is a huge pink bus with 'PROVIDING ACCESSIBLE DENTAL CARE' emblazoned across it.

This was the result of 'Smile for Change,' a partnership with Colgate in 2021, as 40% of rough sleepers endure severe mouth pain. The bus houses a mobile dental clinic (fitted out by fellow social enterprise Community Dental Services).

"What's important is putting yourself in their shoes – what's the benefit for them?" Change Please's founder and CEO, Cemal Ezel, says when asked how he establishes these kinds of partnerships with household name brands (Virgin and Mastercard are some others).

"If you position your offer in line with that benefit, it's a shortcut to them wanting to work for you. Think big and align your values with theirs to ensure it's a partnership that's not going to cause mission drift," is his tip.

As a social enterprise that has been in business for nearly 11 years and works in eight countries, Change Please has proved to be a resilient and successful business. In that time, they've trained 1167 people as baristas (amazingly, more than 100,000 hours of training), giving them a route into work whilst also ensuring all the wraparound support necessary for a stable life is there.

This can include help with accessing food banks or financial services, therapy, or housing advice. Amongst their formerly homeless graduates are refugees, care leavers and ex-prisoners. Not all of them fit the image that comes to mind when we first hear the word 'homelessness' – for some, it's hostels or sofa surfing – but 42% of training graduates have been on the streets.

For all the amazing work they do, we visited their HQ to find out how it felt to win in the Social Enterprise of the Year category at the UK Social Enterprise Awards last year. Having been shortlisted several times before, Cemal was shocked to hear their name announced on the night and, for the first time, hadn't written notes just in case they did – but says the win has already benefitted the business.

"Winning the award has already been an incredible bonus for us because lots of the Buy Social Corporate Challenge partners have already reached out, and we're meeting with them to see how we can partner with them in the longer term," said Cemal. Change Please already supply coffee to many businesses, such as the David Lloyd leisure centres, Avanti West Coast trains and the Department of Work and Pensions. How have Cemal and his team built such a resilient business?

"First and foremost, forget you're a social enterprise. How good is your product in the open market and then, as a bonus, how does it do good? We believe only 4% of organisations or individuals go out of their way to compromise on price, quality and convenience, so if you can focus on making your product as good as it potentially can be and see your social impact as a bonus, you're going to win all day long."

Other organisations similar to Change Please have been established in their wake, something Cemal says he is pleased to see. The competition has motivated them to pivot with new innovations, and the next step is the development of an AI solution to homelessness in partnership with the Cabinet Office.

The app (currently being trialled) signposts to different kinds of support available and allows users to upload official documents in 55 languages, assisting with a variety of required bureaucratic actions. If moving with the times is a sign of a business determined to endure, the longevity of one of the UK's most visible social enterprises looks certain to be extended still further.

                    RUN FOR THE HOMELESS WORLD CUP

        DONATE

Transforming Lives        Changing perceptions        Host CitY Impact        Global Stats        Help us end homelessness

£115 gives one person access to a year-round football programme through one of our Member Countries.

94% of players say the Homeless World Cup positively impacted their lives 88% of players said participation improved their mental health 73% say the Homeless World Cup changed their lives significantly

Experiencing homelessness can be deeply isolating, leaving people disconnected from their communities and without the confidence or support to move forward. Joining a football programme may seem like a small step, but for many it becomes the beginning of something much bigger: a place to belong, build relationships and rediscover a sense of purpose. Across our global network of 75 Member Countries, grassroots organisations use football year-round to provide structure, encouragement and opportunity. Participants are valued for who they are, supported to develop new skills and empowered to shape their own futures. The annual Homeless World Cup is a celebration of that journey. By the time players represent their countries on the global stage, they have already shown extraordinary courage, resilience and determination. Our work also extends beyond the pitch. We connect Member Countries, share knowledge and strengthen collaboration across the network. Through our Cities Ending Homelessness Forum, we bring together city leaders, practitioners, policymakers and people with lived experience to exchange ideas and drive long-term change. Every year, our international network supports more than 220,000 people experiencing homelessness through football. At the tournament, more than 500 players come together for eight days of competition, connection and celebration — showing the world what is possible when people are given the opportunity to thrive.

CHanging Perceptions

75 Member Countries giving us true global reach 71% Of attendees felt more aware of the issues facing people who are homeless after the mexico city 2018 tournament 93% of spectators at the mexico city 2018 homeless world cup said they would recommend going to the event

Explore our year round initiatives

One of the Homeless World Cup Foundation’s core aims is to change the way homelessness is seen, understood and discussed. We shine a light on the people, experiences and potential too often hidden behind the issue — challenging stereotypes and encouraging audiences to look beyond homelessness to the individual. The annual Homeless World Cup is one of the most powerful ways we do this. By placing players with lived experience of homelessness at the centre of a global sporting event, the tournament gives spectators the opportunity to witness their talent, resilience and determination first-hand. Research by La Trobe University found that attending the Homeless World Cup can significantly improve attitudes towards people experiencing homelessness. Seeing players compete, hearing their stories and sharing in their achievements can have a measurable impact on public perceptions. Our work extends far beyond the tournament. Through the Cities Ending Homelessness Forum, we bring together mayors, policymakers, city leaders, practitioners and people with lived experience to share evidence, challenge outdated narratives and explore more effective responses to homelessness. By connecting people with the human reality of homelessness — and bringing cities together to learn, collaborate and act — we help move the conversation beyond stigma and judgement towards empathy, understanding and lasting change.

HOST City impact

Since the first Homeless World Cup in Graz, Austria, in 2003, the tournament has placed host cities at the centre of a global conversation about homelessness, social change and the power of football. From Cape Town and Rio de Janeiro to Paris, Amsterdam, Edinburgh, Sacramento, Seoul and Oslo, each host city has welcomed players, teams and supporters from around the world while showcasing its own culture, character and commitment to positive change. Hosting the tournament creates far more than a sporting moment. It brings communities together, challenges perceptions of homelessness, amplifies the voices of people with lived experience and gives cities a powerful platform to demonstrate leadership on one of the defining social issues of our time. Since 2023, the tournament has also hosted our Cities Ending Homelessness Forum, bringing together mayors, policymakers, practitioners, researchers and global partners to share ideas, strengthen local action and explore long-term solutions. The Forum began in Sacramento, developed further in Seoul and, at the Oslo 2025 Homeless World Cup, became our largest yet — running throughout the eight-day tournament and placing city-level collaboration at the heart of the event. The tournament and the Forum help host cities inspire public action, strengthen partnerships and shine a global light on the conversation around ending homelessness.

18 Major cities have hosted the Homeless World Cup

Join Them - Host a homeless world cup

“The week long tournament in Cardiff provided an incredible platform: for the players to demonstrate their skill and passion; for the public to engage with the tournament and with the varied debate and discussion that sat alongside the football. The Tournament generated a huge amount of positive good will in Cardiff and beyond, and the work to harness this and take it forward into a positive, lasting legacy, has already begun.”

Michael Sheen Homeless World Cup Champion

Global Homelessness Statistics To help our supporters better understand this global problem, we have sought to compile the latest available statistics on the challenges faced by people around the globe.

Why Do We Need Homeless People as City Guides?（impact.info·项目经理谈资金挑战）

来源：https://impact.info/en/why-do-we-need-homeless-people-as-city-guides 抓取时间：2026-11-09

Why Do We Need Homeless People as City Guides? — impact.info

EnglishFrançais English Español Italiano Deutsch

Inclusion Clemens Poldrack

Project Manager — querstadtein e.V.

Why Do We Need Homeless People as City Guides? 10:02·15 October 2026

Listen translateThis article was automatically translated from its original Deutsch version. Read the original Clemens Poldrack is a project manager at querstadtein. This organization offers city tours led by people who have experienced homelessness or have a refugee and migration background.

Berlin is the place that led to the founding of querstadtein e.V. in 2013. The high rate of homelessness is clearly visible but often ignored, as Clemens Poldrack explains. The organization aims to open a dialogue with people who have experienced homelessness through city tours, allowing them to make their experiences visible. These are city tours of a different kind. They do not tell the story of the city, but rather the story of a person from that city. Individuals with experiences of homelessness, displacement, or migration show the city from their perspective, highlighting structural problems along the way. Homelessness has many faces. It is not just about individual fates, but issues that affect everyone: expensive housing, a lack of available apartments, poorly maintained or unrenovated living spaces, insufficient support, and much more. These problems are well-known, but often not seen from the perspective of those affected. Even in 2024, the number of homeless people, according to the Berlin Senate, was still over 55,000. The individual city tours by querstadtein aim to increase the tolerance of fellow citizens and encourage them to take action. The organization, its staff, and the city guides are doing important political education work, Poldrack explains. The educational gap on topics such as homelessness and refugee experiences is still too large. In addition to educational work in the urban landscape, querstadtein also offers workshops. Here, children and teenagers from school classes or other educational institutions prepare the tour and evaluate it afterward. For adults, the organization conducts workshops on homelessness and housing issues. Although the organization has existed for many years, financial resources are still scarce. Therefore, Clemens Poldrack wishes for a financially secure future for the organization that is not solely dependent on grants. Another wish is for more people to engage with the complex issue of housing and homelessness, rather than looking past those affected.

Frequently asked questions What are the main goals of querstadtein e.V.?The main goals of querstadtein e.V. are to make visible the experiences of people with homelessness and flight, as well as to promote a dialogue about these topics. Through individual city tours, participants are encouraged to develop a better understanding of the structural problems of homelessness and to take action.

How do the city tours of querstadtein differ from conventional city tours? What role does education play in the activities of querstadtein? How is the funding of querstadtein secured? In which cities does querstadtein currently offer city tours?

Lena Kirchner impact.info journalist Published on 15 October 2026

流浪者就业caseQuerstadtein-官网

querstadtein e.V. 官网（组织沿革、资助方）

来源：https://querstadtein.org/en?page_id=1524 抓取时间：2026-11-09

querstadtein e.V.

        © querstadtein

On our tours, people who are usually rather talked ABOUT have their say. Our formerly homeless tour guides report about life without a roof over their heads. They present the public spaces and locations where they once lived. New Berliners and new Dresdeners who have fled Syria, Afghanistan and Iraq share their perspectives on their new hometowns.

The tours through different districts and neighbourhoods offer room for dialogue and the opportunity to overcome reservations and reconsider one’s own prejudices.

querstadtein e.V. is a non-profit association. The association aims at promoting popular and professional education according to § 52 section 2 number 7 of the Tax Code. Download the statutes of the association as a PDF file here (in German).

querstadtein e.V. is a member of the Berlin associations AK Wohnungsnot and AK QUEER*WOHNEN.

Our story

In the first year and a half, querstadtein was set up by a team of volunteers. In addition to the association members, the team has since grown to include five permanent employees and a federal volunteer in the office as well as more than twenty tour guides.

    © querstadtein e.V.

  2026 – Deepening educational work and empowerment

In 2026 we place an even stronger focus on younger audiences. Alongside Miriam’s tour for children and young people aged ten and above, we begin developing another tour together with our long-time guide Uwe, designed for children from class 1 onwards – our youngest educational programme to date.

In the ‘Homelessness & Living on the Streets’ section, Yanni is expanding the programme with his guided tour on homelessness from the perspective of a young migrant.

We are also using the prize money from the Hidden Movers Award to further develop our thematic focus on climate and migration.In Kreuzberg, Seif demonstrates how climate change and environmental destruction are exacerbating food shortages and having economic consequences. Segun highlights climate-induced water scarcity, its social consequences and the resulting migration flows. At the same time, we are developing new city tours offering perspectives from India and Israel.

Our long-standing cooperation with the Berliner Sparkasse Foundation continues: once again, 45 Berlin school classes will be able to participate in our walking tours free of charge.

With support from Beisheim Stiftung, we complement our tours with preparatory and follow-up workshops for school classes. In these sessions, pupils engage interactively with the causes and consequences of homelessness and develop ideas for action within their own social environment. The funding also enables free tours for educational institutions from outside of Berlin.

Activities supported by Aktion Mensch are further expanded. New empowerment and educational formats are created, additional intersectional perspectives are incorporated, and our network continues to grow.

In collaboration with Correctiv, we are developing a new section on journalism in exile in Berlin. It will examine the links between critical reporting, persecution and flight, and will focus on how work can be continued in the diaspora. The development of this section is being funded by the German Postcode Lottery as part of a pilot project.

A further project on digital inclusion, also funded by the German Postcode Lottery, supports people with lived experience of homelessness in gaining access to digital technologies and strengthening their participation in society.

It is with mixed feelings that we say goodbye to Susanne, who has flourished in her role as an education officer with us and has been actively involved in self-advocacy for the homeless. She is leaving Berlin to follow her heart.

  2025 – Expanding our educational work and locations

In spring conclude our Climate.Migration.Dialog project. As part of the project, new walking tours are developed that explore the connections between the climate crisis and migration. In Kreuzberg Muhammed’s tour highlights how environmental destruction and global inequality stretch from West Africa to Berlin. Salome’s tour, focusing on Iran, connects questions of affordability, sustainability and social justice. Alongside this, we develop a digital education program on the links between climate change and migration aimed particularly at young people aged 14 and above.

Within our focus area “Berlin migration stories”, we further expand our programme. Sara’s tour explores family migration histories between Colombia and Berlin. Polina brings a queer migrant perspective, speaking about the lives of queer people in Russia, Ukraine and Germany and the importance of community when arriving in a new society. Mahmoud shares his journey from Syria, from resistance against a dictatorship to forced displacement and the search for a new home.

In the area of “Homelessness & Life on the Streets”, a new tour is developed together with Miriam around Ostbahnhof. In it, she examines homelessness from a female and migrant perspective, highlighting the close proximity of tourism, nightlife hotspots, alternative housing forms and often overlooked homelessness.

With support from the Deutsche Stiftung für Engagement und Ehrenamt, we open a new location in Essen. Three educational formats are developed there: Julia’s biographical walking tour focusing on youth homelessness and women, a tour on defensive architecture, and workshops for children and young people on housing insecurity and homelessness.

Our open exhibition space “Here and Now. Women⁺ Experiencing Homelessness in Berlin” remains part of the exhibition BERLIN GLOBAL until June 2025. At the closing event, we look back together on two years of the exhibition.

In 2025, the Stiftung Berliner Sparkasse once again enables 45 Berlin school classes to take part in our walking tours free of charge.

With the Beisheim Stiftung, we gain a new funding partner supporting the development of our educational work on housing insecurity and homelessness. Among other things, we plan a new walking tour for children aged ten and above, accompanied by preparatory and follow-up workshops.

Aktion Mensch also supports the training of additional people with lived experience of homelessness as educational guides. A new collegeau for education and empowerment joins out team.

The work of querstadtein e.V. receives several awards. We are honoured with the Segens Special Prize from the Paul Gerhardt Stift zu Berlin as well as the Hidden Movers Award from the Deloitte Foundation for the project Climate.Migration.Dialogue. In recognition of his many years of commitment, Uwe Tobias receives the Berlin Medal of Honour.

Analog city tours in total: 772 groups

Digital city tours in total: approx. 350 people

    © querstadtein e.V,

    © querstadtein

  2024 — A new topic and a goodbye

We are working intensively on the development of our new focus on climate migration. Through city tours in Berlin and a digital education program, we are drawing attention to the impact of the climate crisis on global refugee and migration movements. The aim is to make the ecological, economic and social consequences of climate change in the countries of origin understandable to a broad audience here in Berlin. The first educational format to be launched is Rose’s city tour, which sheds light on the connection between water, changing weather patterns and migration. Rose places a special focus on the role of women in Kenyan society.

New “Berlin migration stories” complete our program. On Anna’s tour, she talks about fleeing Ukraine as a result of the Russian war of aggression, about what it means to arrive in Berlin and what home is to her. Yauheni, a Belarusian student activist living in exile, offers insights into his immigration experience and discusses the value of a democratic society in relation to the political systems in Germany and Belarus. Maria from Russia talks about her diverse family background and how the war against Ukraine has put a strain on her family’s relationships. Saad gives an insight into Syrian culture and customs along Sonnenallee, which gives him a sense of home. Ragıp introduces the vibrant migrant communities around Kotti, opening up a dialogue on living together and what we need for a peaceful atmosphere in a diverse city.

In the area of “Homelessness & Life on the Street”, Janet adds a new perspective to our program. She is the first female city guide at querstadtein who has been living on the streets herself. She talks about her experiences as a woman in homelessness and the challenges associated with it, such as the omnipresence of violence, and explains why a place in housing alone is not a sufficient solution.

Our open space “Here and Now. Women⁺ Experiencing Homelessness in Berlin!” has established itself as part of the BERLIN GLOBAL exhibition at the Humboldt Forum. Accompanying our open space, which will be on display until June 2025, there will be a supporting program with a reading, tandem tours and other events.

In 2024, we will continue our cooperation with the Joachim Herz Stiftung and the Stiftung Berliner Sparkasse. Thanks to their funding, 90 school classes and other educational institutions will be able to take part in free tours.

With funds from the TransformD funding program of the Deutsche Stiftung für Engagement und Ehrenamt, we are launching a new project entitled “Social cohesion through encounters”, which aims to revise and scale up our educational offerings.

We are delighted to have won the Neukölln Engagement Award. Our city tours in the Neukölln district are honored in the anti-discrimination category.

We say goodbye to our long-time friend, colleague and city guide Klaus Seilwinder at a moving memorial service. He passed away in October 2024 after a long illness and was involved in our organisation until the end. Most recently, he shared his story in a digital city tour. The loss leaves a huge gap and Klaus will always be remembered.

Analog city tours in total: 743 groups

Digital city tours in total: approx. 300 people

  2023 – 10th anniversary of querstadtein

For 10 years querstadtein has been offering city tours that give participants the chance to change their attitude towards minorities. So far around 100,000 people have participated. We are celebrating our anniversary with several smaller and larger events and querstadtein’s work is awarded the Roman Herzog Prize 2023 by Berliner Sparkasse. Our topic has lost none of its political and social relevance. The housing situation is getting worse, and not just in Berlin. On World Homeless Day on October 10th, we discussed the issue critically in an event with 150 guests.

Our ongoing cooperation with the Stiftung Berliner Sparkasse and the Joachim Herz Stiftung is a success and enables us to offer 90 free tours to educational institutions this year. With funding from the “Landeszentrale für politische Bildung Berlin“, we develop workshops for school classes on the topic “Right to housing — injustice of homelessness”.

Three new city tours start: Salome talks about the contradictions of Tehran and Berlin and draws attention to lived realities. Susanne shares her perspective on poverty among women in old age, cultural participation and homelessness. Thomas reports on the causes of homelessness and the effects of forced evictions.

In cooperation with the foundation “Stadtmuseum Berlin” we develop an exhibition “Here and now. Women* experiencing homelessness in Berlin” as part of BERLIN GLOBAL at the Humboldt Forum. The space opens on the 20th October and will remain open until 31 March 2025.

With funding from the EU program “REACT with impact”, we work intensively with the consulting agency “zukunft zwei” to develop the strategic direction for the coming years. Among other things, we plan to expand our formats and to develop new content. The “Climate.Migration.Dialogue” project funded by the Deutsche Postcode Lotterie is launched in the second half of the year.

Total analog city tours: 684 groups

Total digital city tours: approx. 675 people

    © querstadtein

    © querstadtein/Anna Rozkosny

  2022 — New perspectives

At the beginning of the year, Jennifer Fielding takes over as managing director.

Two of our projects are aimed at children and young people: The Joachim Herz Foundation ensures that even more schoolchildren can benefit from our children’s tours on migration and homelessness. The Stiftung Berliner Sparkasse is also sponsoring guided tours for school classes in Berlin, which will be able to take part in our educational program free of charge again this year.

We are preparing an exhibition contribution about “Women* and queer people in housing emergencies” for the exhibition BERLIN GLOBAL at the Stadtmuseum.

New tours are being planned — we are working on a focus on climate migration and postcolonial perspectives on migration and the city of Berlin.

At the end of the year, Katrin Elsemann joins the board. We thank Silvine Gerlach-Höbing for her many years of service as a board member.

Uwe celebrates his 1,000th tour with long-time companions and accompanied by press reporters.

The MONOM Stiftung supports us with the “Kleine Hilfe” grant, with which we can pay an inflation bonus to all our employees.

Muhammed Lamin’s tour “Kreuzberg behind the Scenes: The Making of a Diverse Neighborhood” is awarded in the 2022 “Aktiv für Demokratie und Toleranz” competition.

Total analog city tours: 537 groups

Total digital city tours: approx. 1100 people

  2021 — New beginnings

The year has started with a lot of energy and a new project! Thanks to funding from the Postcode Lottery we can work on ensuring that our tours are pandemic-proof. We are improving our digital offerings and are looking for new methods and target groups.

The aim: For the personal stories of our city guides about forced migration, displacement and homelessness to reach an even larger audience.

As part of the network Migration Lab we are able to connect to other stakeholders from science and other peers, in order to develop projects together. We are supported by the Foundation Stiftung Erinnerung, Verantwortung, Zukunft.

    Stadtführer Klaus führt eine Gruppe mit Abstand und Masken

    © querstadtein

    Der Audiowalk "Stimmen vom Bahnhof Zoo"

    © querstadtein/Ilona Marti

  2020 — SURVIVING THE PANDEMIC THROUGH CREATIVE INNOVATION

Unfortunately, more than half of our tours were cancelled. So instead, we have developed innovative, digital formats in order to continue to reach a large number of people. As part of the digital city tour on the topic of “stories of contract workers”, we take a critical look at the situation of contract workers from Vietnam and Mozambique who came to Saxony, as well at the issues that still affect their children´s generation today. Our new digital tour about “Life on the Streets” is independent of any specific location. At the end of these online seminars there are personal encounters with the city tour guides.

Together with the Berliner Stadtmission and supported by the Deutsche Bahn Stiftung, we are developing the audio tour Stimmen vom Bahnhof Zoo / Voices from Bahnhof Zoo Train Station: At different locations from Bahnhof Zoo to Ku´Damm avenue, Jenny, Erhard, Anton and Blümchen describe their daily lives without their own home.

Two new city tours are starting: Abuhanna shares his views about the vibrant neighbourhood Neukölln. On Muhammed’s tour, you can learn about the upheavals in the European asylum system and how old and new Berliners shape their neighborhoods.

Total analog city tours: 243

Total digital city tours: 332

  2019 — querstadtein continues to grow

We develop new tours on both topics homelessness and flight & asylum. In Dresden, we conduct city tours especially for young people from Saxony in the project #angekommen (supported by the state programme Weltoffenes Sachsen für Demokratie und Toleranz) and develop accompanying material with lesson plans.

With the support of the Berliner Sparkasse Foundation we can offer 50 free tours for pupils from Berlin this year. An absolute highlight: our city tours on the subject of homelessness for pupils are awarded with the David-Preis of the Sparkassen-Finanzgruppe and the Deutscher Engagement-Preis.

As scholarship holders in the openTransfer Accelerator programme of the Stiftung Bürgermut, we are working on scaling up.

Total number of city tours: 770

    Dr Richard Lutz, CEO Deutsche Bahn AG, awarding Uwe Tobias and Dieter Bichler (f.l.t.r.)

    ©Svea Pietschmann

    Tour with Mohamad Nhar (Dresden)

    © querstadtein/Valeska Hoischen

  2018 – querstadtein goes Dresden

The bpb funding continues for the third year running – a great opportunity to grow beyond the borders of Berlin: In late April, the project Refugees show their Dresden is launched. Furthermore, with the support of the Berliner Sparkasse Foundation we are able to organize 45 free homeless tours for school classes from Berlin.

querstadtein is awarded Landmark in the Land of Ideas and shortlisted for the FARBENBEKENNEN-Award initiated by the Senate Chancellery Berlin.

In August the association’s name is officially changed to querstadtein e.V.

Total city tours: 673

  2017 – The founders hand over the baton

Mohamad Khalil now offers the tour through Neukölln in German. Wael Alafandi adds a refugee tour through Berlin city centre. Meanwhile, the office grows into a three-person team.

The founders hand over the baton to a new management team: In May, new board members Sandra Brandt, Andreas Schlamm and Marvin Wiek take up their post. In September, Selina Byfield joins querstadtein as Managing Director.

Total city tours: 628.

    Uwe Tobias, Sally Ollech, Kerstin Blumberg and Klaus Seilwinder (f. l. t. r.)

    © querstadtein

    Tour with Firas Zakri

    © querstadtein

  2016 – The new city tours begin

As of March, guided city tours are funded as a model project by the Federal Agency for Civic Education. Samer Serawan, Arij Oudeh and Firas Zakri are the first tour guides from Syria to offer tours through Neukölln in English. They are soon joined by Ahmad Oudeh and Hamdi al Kasar. Nafee Kurdis’ tour through Berlin city centre starts in December.

Total city tours: 417.

  2015 – New tours and a new theme

Together with Uwe Tobias, the team develops a city tour for children. In July, Klaus Seilwinder makes his tour debut. querstadtein is honoured by the Robert Bosch Foundation within the programme Die Verantwortlichen (The Responsibles).

With Refugees show their Berlin, querstadtein establishes a second group of city guides. This wins them the scholarship ANKOMMER. Perspektive Deutschland (together with the Sharehaus Refugio). The office now moves to its new location in the Refugio.

Total city tours: 208.

    Tour with Hamdi Kassar

    © querstadtein

    Tour with Dieter Bichler

    © querstadtein/Mathias Becker

  2014 – A first foundation provides funding

At the end of the first year, querstadtein can look back upon some 3,000 participants. In March, the first full-time position is created with funds of the Auerbach Foundation. At events discussing the problem of homelessness, the team contributes its expertise; querstadtein becomes a member of the AK Wohnungsnot, a Berlin-based committee on housing problems.

Dieter Bichler joins the city guides with a tour through the neighbourhood City-West. The feedback of the tour participants increasingly shows us that querstadtein succeeds in changing their attitude and behaviour towards homeless people in everyday life.

  2013 – Homeless people show their Berlin

querstadtein wins the startsocial scholarship which implies free advice and counselling. In March, 13 team members found the association Stadtsichten e.V. and launch cooperations with players such as the Berliner Stadtmission and the homeless magazine strassenfeger. The first tour starts in June with city guide Carsten Voss. He is later joined by Uwe Tobias.

For this project, the team of volunteers is awarded the FES prize for social commitment. At the same time, the founders continue to develop querstadtein as a social enterprise: At the Social Impact Start Pitch, they win a free coaching and two places in the Social Impact Lab.

    Uwe Tobias and Carsten Voss

    © querstadtein/Mathias Becker

    Founders Katharina Kühn and Sally Ollech

    Mathias Becker

  2012 – An idea takes shape

Our founders Katharina Kühn and Sally Ollech observe the gulf between homeless people and mainstream society. They want to bring the two groups in contact. Their idea: formerly homeless people give tours through the streets that were once their home. The aim is to give first-hand insights into the situation of those affected by homelessness, to reduce prejudices, to create a platform for encounters and to provide previously homeless people with an additional income.

The two founders submit their concept to the competition startsocial, which supports social projects with economic know-how, and begin to implement their project.

Book now

Our guides work throughout the year, come rain or shine.

Walks typically last around 2 hours.

Tickets for our public tours are £18 per full-priced attendee, £15 for concessions, and children under 15 are only £1 with a paying adult.

Groups are typically between 8-12 people.

Get in touch if you’d like to book a private tour – great for team building and educational activities!

Buy now

Why book with Unseen Tours?

Our brilliant guides, being formerly homeless, know the streets intimately and will show you the real London through their stories and unmatched knowledge of the area’s history.

Your ticket helps our guides to earn a living doing something that they love. It also gives them the opportunity to lead conversations that challenge perceptions about homelessness and generate social change.
